Frames of iterations and vector-valued model spaces
Carlos Cabrelli, Ursula Molter, Daniel Suárez

Abstract
. Let T T be a bounded operator on a Hilbert space ℋ \mathcal{H} , and ℱ = { f j , j ∈ J } \mathcal{F}=\{f_{j},j\in J\} an at most countable set of vectors in ℋ \mathcal{H} . In this note we characterize the pairs ( T , ℱ ) (T,\mathcal{F}) such that { T n f : f ∈ ℱ , n ∈ I } \{T^{n}f:f\in\mathcal{F},n\in I\} form a frame of ℋ \mathcal{H} , for the cases of I = ℕ ∪ { 0 } I=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\} and I = ℤ I=\mathbb{Z} . The characterization for unilateral iterations gives a similarity with the compression of the shift acting on model spaces of the Hardy space of analytic functions defined on the unit disk with values in ℓ 2 ​ ( J ) \ell^{2}(J) . This generalizes recent work for iterations of a single function. In the case of bilateral iterations the characterization is by the bilateral shift acting on doubly invariant subspaces of L 2 ​ ( T , ℓ 2 ​ ( J ) ) L^{2}(T,\ell^{2}(J)) . Furthermore, we characterize the frames of iterations for vector-valued model operators when J J is finite in terms of Toeplitz and multiplication operators in the unilateral and bilateral case, respectively. Finally we study the problem of finding the minimal number of orbits that produce a frame in this context. For the unilateral case we proved a formula for a lower bound. We conjecture that this lower bound is sharp. We give a new proof in the bilateral case, for which a formula is known.



1.
Introduction

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
a bounded linear operator on
\mathcal{H}
and

\{x_{j}:\,j\in J\}\subset\mathcal{H}
be a subset of
\mathcal{H}
, where the set of indexes
J
is
countable (finite or countably infinite). A natural problem originated in dynamical sampling asks for
a characterization of the operators and the vectors such that the orbits

\mathcal{F}=\{T^{n}x_{j}:\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ j\in J\}

is a frame, or even a Bessel sequence for
\mathcal{H}
, where
\mathbb{N}_{0}
denotes the set of nonnegative integers.

When
T
is a normal operator and
\sharp J=1
(the cardinal of
J
) the problem was solved in two papers

[
2
]
and
[
1
]
, where the authors showed that
T
is necessarily diagonalizable, the diagonal is
an interpolating sequence for the Hardy space of the unit disk
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})

(including finite interpolation if
\dim\mathcal{H}
is finite),
and the vector
x
must satisfy some bound constraints depending on the eigenvalues of
T
.

In
[
5
]
the same problem is studied for an arbitrary finite set
J
. In this case the normal operator
T
also must be diagonalizable, where the diagonal
is a union of at most
\sharp J
interpolating sequences. A refinement of this characterization was obtained in
[
6
]
, where the authors show that hyperbolic geometry appears naturally in the interplay
between the vectors
x_{j}\ (j\in J)
and the sequence of eigenvalues.

In
[
8
]
the authors consider the case of an arbitrary bounded operator
T
,

not necessarily normal
, and the iterations of a single vector
f\in\mathcal{H}

i.e.
\#J=1
.
They showed that the orbit
\{T^{n}f:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
is a frame of
\mathcal{H}
if and only if
there exist a model space
K_{\theta}
of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
, and a bounded invertible operator
V
,
from
K_{\theta}
to
\mathcal{H}

such that
T
is similar to
S_{\theta}
, i.e.
T=VS_{\theta}V^{-1}.
Here
S_{\theta}
is the compression of the unilateral shift acting on
K_{\theta},
and

K_{\theta}=H^{2}\ominus\theta H^{2}
with
\theta
an inner function.
Furthermore, they show that there exists a function
\varphi\in K_{\theta}
with
V\varphi=f
such that
\{S^{n}_{\theta}\varphi:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
is a frame of
K_{\theta}.

In other words, that theorem says that the orbit of a bounded operator in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}

forms a frame if and only if the orbit of the compression of the shift in
some model space isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}
forms a frame of the model space.
The authors also obtain similar results for the case of orbits in
\mathbb{Z}
,
and the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
for the characterization.

In the present paper we show that these results can be extended to the case of countable orbits.
For this we need to use model spaces in Hardy spaces with multiplicity.
We also generalized the case of
\mathbb{Z}-
orbits, with the bilateral shift and reducing subspaces
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J)).

On the other hand, this characterization motivates the study of frames of model spaces,
consisting of orbits
of the compression of the shift.
Specifically, we consider the following problems.

For a given model space
\mathcal{N}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
, characterize all the sets
\{\varphi_{j}:j\in J\}\subset\mathcal{N}

such that
\{S_{\mathcal{N}}^{n}\varphi_{j}:j\in J,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
is a frame of
\mathcal{N}.
Here
S_{\mathcal{N}}

is the compression of the shift to
\mathcal{N}.
We obtain necessary and sufficient conditions
in Theorem
2.8
. We also address the same problem for
T
invertible and
\mathbb{Z}
-orbits, for which we find the necessary and sufficient conditions in Theorem
3.4
,
and determine the minimum number of orbits needed to obtain a frame for
\mathcal{H}
.

Finally,
given a bounded operator
T
in a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
which is the minimum cardinality of a set of functions
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{H}

such that
\{T^{n}f:f\in\mathcal{A},n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
is a frame of
\mathcal{H}
?
Using the results mentioned above, this question can be studied in model spaces of some Hardy space. In this case we provide a conjecture (Conjecture
2.14
) together with an example supporting it.

The paper is organized as follows:

After establishing the necessary notation and definitions, in Section
2
we
address the unilateral case. We first establish in Theorem
2.1
a characterization of
when the forward iterations of a bounded operator on a set
\{f_{j},j\in J\}
in an arbitrary Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
are a frame of
\mathcal{H}
.

This motivates in Subsection
2.1
the study of frames for the unilateral shift
of multiplicity
\sharp J
acting on co-invariant subspaces of
H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
.
In Subsection
2.2
we address the question of establishing the minimum cardinality
of unilateral orbits needed to form a frame.

Finally in Section
3
we look at doubly invariant subspaces, for which in Theorem
3.5
we establish the minimum number of orbits needed to obtain a frame for a doubly invariant subspace of of
(L^{2})^{m}
.

2.
Unilateral orbits

Let
J
be a countable set of indexes (i.e.: finite or infinite denumerable) and
denote by
H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
the Hardy space of analytic functions from the
torus
\mathbb{T}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\!:|z|=1\}
into
\ell^{2}(J)
. That is, any
g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))

can be written as

(1)

g(z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}z^{n},\mbox{ where $a_{n}\in\ell^{2}(J)$ and }\|g\|^{2}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\|a_{n}\|_{\ell^{2}(J)}^{2}.

Via this representation we can think of
\ell^{2}(J)
as a closed subspace of

H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
.
Denote by
S
the operator on
H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
of multiplication by
z

(the unilateral shift of multiplicity
\sharp J
). If
E\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
is
a closed subspace and
P_{E}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto
E
, the operator

S_{E}:=P_{E}S|_{E}

is called the compression of
S
to
E
.
We say that
E
is coinvariant for
S

if its orthogonal complement
E^{\bot}
is
S
-invariant.
In this case,
E
is called a model space and
S_{E}
a model operator.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
f_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, where
j\in J
is countable.
Then

(2)

\{T^{n}f_{j}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ j\in J\}

is a frame for
\mathcal{H}
if and only if there is an
S
-coinvariant subspace

K\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
and an invertible operator

V:K\to\mathcal{H}
such that

V^{-1}TV=S_{K}\ \mbox{ and }\ V(P_{K}e_{j})=f_{j}\ \mbox{ for all $j\in J$},

where
e_{j}\in\ell^{2}(J)
is the standard basis.

Proof.

Since
\{S^{n}e_{j}:\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in J\}
is an ortonormal basis of

H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
,
we can define a linear transformation
W:H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))\to\mathcal{H}
by setting

(3)

W(S^{n}e_{j})=T^{n}f_{j},\ \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in J.

The operator
W
is then bounded because (
2
) is a Bessel sequence and it is onto because
this sequence is a frame. Therefore, letting
K:=(\mbox{Ker}\,W)^{\bot}
, we have that

W|_{K}:K\to\mathcal{H}
is invertible.
In addition, it is clear from (
3
) that
TW=WS
, implying that
\mbox{Ker}\,W
is
S
-invariant,
which means that
K
is coinvariant. Consider the
compression
S_{K}
of
S
to
K
. We claim that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W|_{K}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W|_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{K}}

First notice that
W|_{K}P_{K}(S^{n}e_{j})=W(S^{n}e_{j})=T^{n}f_{j}
. Hence, applying
T
we get

TW|_{K}P_{K}(S^{n}e_{j})=T^{n+1}f_{j}=W|_{K}P_{K}(S^{n+1}e_{j}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle W|_{K}^{-1}TW|_{K}P_{K}(S^{n}e_{j})

\displaystyle=P_{K}(S^{n+1}e_{j})=(P_{K}S)(S^{n}e_{j})

\displaystyle=(P_{K}S)(P_{K}S^{n}e_{j})+(P_{K}S)(P_{K^{\bot}}S^{n}e_{j})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}(P_{K}S)(P_{K}S^{n}e_{j}),

where (1) holds because
K^{\bot}
is
S
-invariant. Since
P_{K}S^{n}e_{j}

generate
K
, this proves our claim. Thus, the operator
V:=W|_{K}
satisfies the theorem.
∎

Now, we have the following proposition whose proof is immediate.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{0},\,\mathcal{H}
be Hilbert spaces,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
f_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, for
j\in J
countable.
If
V:\mathcal{H}_{0}\to\mathcal{H}
is an invertible operator, then

\{T^{n}f_{j}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ j\in J\}\hskip 5.69054pt\mbox{is a frame for $\mathcal{H}$}

if and only if
\displaystyle\,\{(V^{-1}TV)^{n}(V^{-1}f_{j}):n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ j\in J\}

is a frame for
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.

Thus, combining Proposition
2.2
with Theorem
2.1
we have the following corollary.

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in J}
a set of vectors in
\mathcal{H}
.The following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

\{T^{n}f_{j}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ j\in J\}\hskip 5.69054pt\mbox{is a frame for $\mathcal{H}$}
.

(2)

There exists an
S
-covariant space
K
in
H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
, a collection of functions
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j\in J}\subset K
and, an isomorphism
V:K\to\mathcal{H}
such that

\{S_{K}^{n}\varphi_{j}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ j\in J\}\hskip 5.69054pt\mbox{is a frame for $K$}

with
V^{-1}TV=S_{K}\ \mbox{ and }\ V(\varphi_{j})=f_{j}\ \mbox{ for all }j\in J
.

In short, what Corollary
2.3
says is that every frame formed by iterations of a bounded operator
applied to a countable set of a Hilbert space can be
represented as a frame of iterations of a model operator.

2.1.
Toeplitz operators on model spaces

In this section we will only consider the case where the set
J
of Theorem
2.1
is finite.
By that theorem we can transfer the study of frames from an abstract context to the more familiar
scheme of model spaces. There we have many powerful tools from operator and function theory that we
can use to characterize Bessel sequences and frames of orbits. After doing so, we can go back to the original setting
by the invertible operator
V
that implements the similarity in Theorem
2.1
.

One of the main tools at our disposal are Toeplitz operators with matricial symbols. In this section we shall
see that the orbits generated by a finite set
J
form a Bessel sequence or a frame if an only if
a certain associated Toeplitz operator is bounded or bounded above and below (respectively). Furthermore, we find
simple necessary and sufficient conditions for the respective boundedness of this operator.

For
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
L^{2}=L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and a matrix
G\in(L^{2})^{m\times k}
let

where

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle h\hfil

is taken as a column vector.
Then for any linear submanifold

we can define a Toeplitz operator

by the rule

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle h\hfil$\crcr}}}),

where
P_{(H^{2})^{m}}
is the orthogonal projection.
Observe that
D_{G}
is dense in
(H^{2})^{k}
, since it contains
(H^{\infty})^{k}
.
If we write
G=(g_{ij})
it follows easily that
T_{G}
extends boundedly to
(H^{2})^{k}
if and only if
all the scalar operators
T_{g_{ij}}
extend boundedly to
H^{2}
, which is equivalent to
g_{ij}\in L^{\infty}

for all
i,j
(see
[
21
, Prop. 10.1]
).
However, if
T_{G}
is defined on a linear submanifold
D\subset D_{G}
,
the above condition could be unnecessary for extending
T_{G}
continuously to the closure of
D
.
If
G=(g_{ij})\in(L^{2})^{m\times k}
write
G^{\ast}:=(\overline{g}_{ji})\in(L^{2})^{k\times m}

for the conjugate transpose matrix. A simple calculation shows that

,
the Toeplitz operator

2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle h\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle h\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle h\hfil$\crcr}}}\in(H^{\infty})^{m}
is densely defined in
(H^{2})^{m}
.
The (unilateral) shift
S
of multiplicity
m\in\mathbb{N}
is the Toeplitz
operator with symbol
zI_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}
acting on
(H^{2})^{m}
.
That is,
S
multiplies each coordinate of
h\in(H^{2})^{m}
by the variable
z
. It is clear that

ST_{\Theta}=T_{\Theta}S
when
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
, implying that the
closure of
T_{\Theta}(H^{2})^{m}
is a closed
S
-invariant subspace.
The Beurling-Lax-Halmos Theorem
(for the respective scalar
[
3
]
, finite dimensional
[
16
]
and infinite dimensional

[
12
]
case) says that all the
S
-invariant subspaces are of this form and
they can be written in a more precise and systematic form.

A matrix
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
is called rigid if the non-tangential limits

\Theta(e^{it})
are partial isometries on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
with the same initial space for almost
every point
e^{it}\in\mathbb{T}
. We include the null matrix as a particular case,
and we point out that when the initial space is all of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
the matrix is called inner.
It follows that
T_{\Theta}(H^{2})^{m}
is closed and therefore an
S
-invariant subspace.
The B-L-H Theorem says that these are all.
Consequently, all the
S
-coinvariant subspaces (i.e.:
S^{\ast}
-invariant) have the form

K_{\Theta}:=(H^{2})^{m}\ominus T_{\Theta}(H^{2})^{m}

for a rigid matrix
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
.
As stated before in a more abstract setting, if
P_{K_{\Theta}}=I-T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto
K_{\Theta}
, the
operator obtained by compressing
S
to this space:

S_{\Theta}:=P_{K_{\Theta}}S\big|_{K_{\Theta}}:K_{\Theta}\to K_{\Theta}

is called a model operator, while
K_{\Theta}
is a called a model space.
Since polynomials
\mathcal{P}^{m}
are dense in
(H^{2})^{m}
, the linear manifold

(I-T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}})(\mathcal{P}^{m})\subset(H^{\infty})^{m}\cap K_{\Theta}\ \mbox{ is dense in $K_{\Theta}$}.

Therefore, when

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , the restriction of

.

that make it bounded.

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle h\hfil$\crcr}}}\in K_{\Theta}
.
Now we can use a powerful tool of operator theory, the Sz.-Nagy and Foias
Commutant Lifting Theorem (see
[
9
, Thm. 5]
), allowing us to lift the operator

T_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\ast}}|_{K_{\Theta}}
to a bounded operator on
(H^{2})^{m}
that also satisfies the
intertwining relation.
So, assuming boundedness, the mentioned theorem
tells us that there is a bounded operator
L:(H^{2})^{m}\to H^{2}
such that

L|_{K_{\Theta}}=T_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\ast}}|_{K_{\Theta}}\ \mbox{ and }\ LS^{\ast}_{m}=S^{\ast}_{1}L\mbox{ on $(H^{2})^{m}$}.

Hence, the restriction of
L
to each coordinate of
(H^{2})^{m}
is a bounded operator on
H^{2}
that
commutes with

S^{\ast}_{1}
, meaning that
L=T_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern for some

we see that

Hence, by (
5
) the family

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}_{j}:\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\ 1\leq j\leq k\}

is a Bessel sequence in
K_{\Theta}
if and only if
T_{G^{\ast}}\big|_{K_{\Theta}}
is bounded,
and is a frame if and only if it is bounded above and below.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
F\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times k}
and
\Theta
be a rigid matrix. Then

T_{F}T_{F^{\ast}}\geq\delta^{2}I_{(H^{2})^{m}}\mbox{ on }K_{\Theta}\hskip 8.53581pt\mbox{ for some $\delta>0$}

if and only if

\displaystyle\ \|T_{F^{\ast}}h\|+\|T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|\geq\varepsilon\|h\|

for all
h\in(H^{2})^{m}
and some
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

The implication (
\Leftarrow
) follows by noticing that
\mbox{Ker}\,T_{\Theta^{\ast}}=K_{\Theta}
.
To prove (
\Rightarrow
) we can assume that
\|T_{F^{\ast}}\|\leq 1
.
If
h\in(H^{2})^{m}
has norm 1,

\displaystyle\delta\big[1-\|T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|\big]

\displaystyle\leq\delta\|(I-T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}})h\|\ \leq\|T_{F^{\ast}}(I-T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}})h\|

\displaystyle\leq\ \|T_{F^{\ast}}h\|+\|T_{F^{\ast}}T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|\ \leq\|T_{F^{\ast}}h\|+\|T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|,

where the first inequality follows from
\|T_{\Theta}\|\leq 1
and the second inequality holds by hypothesis.
If
\|T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|\leq 1/2
this proves that

\delta/2\leq\|T_{F^{\ast}}h\|+\|T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|
.
Otherwise
\|T_{\Theta^{\ast}}h\|>1/2
and there is nothing to prove.
∎

The next Proposition requires a version of the Matrix Corona Theorem proved independently by Fuhrmann

[
10
, Thm. 3.1]
and Vasyunin (credited in
[
20
, Thm. 3]
).

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
F_{j}\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}

for
1\leq j\leq q
. Then there are
B_{j}\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{q}F_{j}(z)B_{j}(z)=I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\ \ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{D}.

if and only if there is some
\eta>0
such that

\,\sum_{j=1}^{q}F_{j}(z)F^{\ast}_{j}(z)\geq\eta^{2}I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}
,

\ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{D}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
be a rigid matrix and
F\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times k}
.
The following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

T_{F}T_{F^{\ast}}\geq\delta^{2}I_{(H^{2})^{m}}
on
K_{\Theta}
for some
\delta>0
.

(2)

F(z)F^{\ast}(z)+\Theta(z)\Theta^{\ast}(z)\geq\eta^{2}I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

for some
\eta>0
and all
z\in\mathbb{D}
.

(3)

There exist
A\in(H^{\infty})^{k\times m}
and
B\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
such that

F(z)A(z)+\Theta(z)B(z)=I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\ \ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{D}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
, condition (1) says that there is some
\varepsilon>0
such that

(6)

T_{F}T_{F^{\ast}}+T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}}\geq\varepsilon^{2}I_{(H^{2})^{m}}.

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
z\in\mathbb{D}
, which is (2) with
\eta=\varepsilon
.

Now suppose that (2) holds. If
k=m
then (3) follows immediately from Theorem
2.6
.
Otherwise the idea is to use a very simple trick of linear algebra to reduce our problem to that theorem.
Write
k=mp+r
, where
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\,0\leq r<m
, and decompose the matrix
F(z)
into

p
square matrices
F_{j}(z)\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
formed by each of the columns of
F(z)
, and
if
r>0
include another matrix
F_{p+1}(z)
formed by the last
r
columns of
F(z)
plus
columns of zeros until completing a
(m\times m)
-matrix. Assume this case to avoid
changing indexes. Then condition (2) translates into

\sum_{j=1}^{p+1}F_{j}(z)F^{\ast}_{j}(z)+\Theta(z)\Theta^{\ast}(z)\geq\eta^{2}I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}},\ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{D}.

Thus, Theorem
2.6
says that there are matrices
A_{j},B\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{p+1}F_{j}(z)A_{j}(z)+\Theta(z)B(z)=I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}},\ \ \ \forall z\in\mathbb{D}.

It is clear then that if
A\in(H^{\infty})^{k\times m}
is formed by all the files of
A_{1}
followed by
the files of
A_{2}
and so on until the first
r
files of
A_{p+1}
, then
A
and
B
satisfy (3).

Finally, if (3) holds, passing to Toeplitz operators we get

I_{(H^{2})^{m}}=T_{FA+\Theta B}^{\ast}=(T_{F}T_{A}+T_{\Theta}T_{B})^{\ast}=T_{A^{\ast}}T_{F^{\ast}}+T_{B^{\ast}}T_{\Theta^{\ast}},

which implies that
T_{F^{\ast}}|_{K_{\Theta}}
has a left inverse, and this is equivalent to (1).

∎

Theorem 2.8
.

be a rigid matrix and

such that

for

.
So, writing

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}_{k}\big]\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times k}
, a new use of
Lemma
2.4
gives

T_{G^{\ast}}\big|_{K_{\Theta}}=T_{F^{\ast}}\big|_{K_{\Theta}}.

Thus,
T_{G^{\ast}}\big|_{K_{\Theta}}
is bounded below if and only if

T_{F^{\ast}}\big|_{K_{\Theta}}
satisfies (1) of Propos.
2.7
.
∎

Among the conditions of Proposition
2.7
it is apparent that (2) is the easiest to check.
This fact will be reflected in the next two examples, where it is used to characterize frames
of orbits in accordance with Theorem
2.8
.

Example 2.9
.

Suppose that
u\in H^{2}
is inner or the null function and let
f_{j}\in H^{\infty}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
,
be such that

|f_{1}(z)|^{2}+\cdots+|f_{k}(z)|^{2}+|u(z)|^{2}\geq\eta^{2}

for some
\eta>0
and all
z\in\mathbb{D}
, which is (2) of Proposition
2.7
.
Then the
S_{u}
orbits of

g_{j}:=(I-T_{u}T_{\overline{u}})f_{j}\in K_{u}\ \mbox{ for }\ j=1,\ldots k

form a frame for
K_{u}
. By the last theorem any frame of at most
k
orbits
(because of possible repetitions) has this form. Repetitions of the
g_{j}^{\prime}s
can be avoided
by setting the additional condition
f_{i}-f_{j}\not\in uH^{\infty}
for
1\leq i<j\leq m
.
Furthermore, if the set of functions
\{f_{j}\!:1\leq j\leq k\}
that satisfies the above inequality is
minimal then so is
\{g_{j}\!:1\leq j\leq k\}
, in the sense that if we extract any of the respective orbits,
the remaining orbits no longer form a frame.

When
u\equiv 0
the corresponding operator is the simple shift
S
and a single
orbit is a frame if and only if it is generated by an invertible function
f\in\mbox{$H^{\infty}$}
.
Observe the contrast with the characterization of cyclic vectors for
S
given by Beurling’s theorem,
where the orbit is generated by an outer function
g\in H^{2}
(see
[
11
, Cor. 7.3]
).

Example 2.10
.

For
b_{1},b_{2}\in H^{\infty}
inner functions such that

\inf_{\mathbb{D}}|b_{1}|^{2}+|b_{2}|^{2}\geq\delta^{2}
consider the inner matrix

\Theta=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}&0\\
0&b_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}\in(H^{\infty})^{2\times 2}

and the model operator
S_{\Theta}:K_{\Theta}\subset(H^{2})^{2}\to K_{\Theta}
.
If
F={1\choose 1}\in(H^{\infty})^{2\times 1}
then

\displaystyle\left\|F^{\ast}(z){c_{1}\choose c_{2}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\Theta^{\ast}(z){c_{1}\choose c_{2}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=|c_{1}+c_{2}|^{2}+|b_{1}(z)c_{1}|^{2}+|b_{2}(z)c_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(|c_{1}|-|c_{2}|)^{2}+\delta^{2}\min\{|c_{1}|^{2},|c_{2}|^{2}\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}\,(|c_{1}|^{2}+|c_{2}|^{2})

for all
(c_{1},c_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
z\in\mathbb{D}
, where the last inequality follows easily
by considering the two cases
2|c_{2}|\leq|c_{1}|
and
2|c_{2}|\geq|c_{1}|\geq|c_{2}|
.
Since the above inequality is (2) of Proposition
2.7
,
then Theorem
2.8
says that the
S_{\Theta}
-orbit generated by

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}:=(I-T_{\Theta}T_{\Theta^{\ast}}){1\choose 1}={1-b_{1}\overline{b_{1}(0)}\choose 1-b_{2}\overline{b_{2}(0)}}\in K_{\Theta}

is a frame for
K_{\Theta}
. Therefore
K_{\Theta}
admits a frame of a single
S_{\Theta}
-orbit, which according
to Theorem
2.1
means that
S_{\Theta}
is similar to a scalar model operator.
We can imitate the proof of that theorem to find the model space and the similarity.
Indeed, let
W:H^{2}\to K_{\Theta}
be the linear operator defined by

W(z^{n})=S^{n}_{\Theta}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern Hence,

\displaystyle W(h)=P_{K_{\Theta}}{h\choose h}
for every

, and consequently

So,
(\text{Ker}\,W)^{\bot}=K_{b_{1}b_{2}}
, the operator

is invertible,

V^{-1}S_{\Theta}V=S_{b_{1}b_{2}}\ \ \mbox{ and }\ \ VP_{K_{b_{1}b_{2}}}1=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}.

2.2.
Minimum cardinality of unilateral orbits

Definition.

Let
T:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
be a bounded operator on a
separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
We define the (unilateral) frame number
\vartheta_{+}(T)
of
T

as the minimum

m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
such that there exists a frame formed by the union of
m
orbits.
If there is no
m
with this property we say that
T
has no unilateral frame number.
If
T
is invertible, its (bilateral) frame number
\vartheta_{-}(T)
is defined analogously
with the bilateral orbits
\{T^{n}g:\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, for
g\in\mathcal{H}
.

Observe that Theorem
2.1
implies that
T
has frame number
m
if and only if
it is similar to a model operator
S_{\Theta}
acting on
K_{\Theta}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
,
where
m=\sharp J
is the minimum with this property.
Examples
2.9
and
2.10
show two operators of frame number 1.
A simple example will show that a rank 1 operator could have infinite frame number or no number at all.

Example 2.11
.

Every rank 1 operator on the separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
has the form

Tx=\langle x,g\rangle f
for all
x\in\mathcal{H}
, where
f,g\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{0\}
. Thus,

T^{0}x=x\ \mbox{ and }\ T^{n}x=\langle x,g\rangle\,\langle f,g\rangle^{n-1}f,\/\ \mbox{ for $n\in\mathbb{N}$. }

Therefore

\sum_{n\geq 0}|\langle h,T^{n}x\rangle|^{2}=|\langle h,x\rangle|^{2}+|\langle x,g\rangle|^{2}\,|\langle h,f\rangle|^{2}\,\sum_{n\geq 1}|\langle f,g\rangle|^{2(n-1)}.

If
|\langle f,g\rangle|\geq 1
, the series diverges, so when
x\not\perp g
the orbit of
x
is not even
a Bessel sequence (take
h=f
). When
x\bot g
, taking
h=g
we see that
\langle g,T^{n}x\rangle=0
for all
n
and there
is no frame formed by orbits.
Finally, if
|\langle f,g\rangle|<1
and
\{x_{j}:j\in J\}
is a frame, then so is

\{T^{n}x_{j}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,j\in J\}
. That is,
\vartheta_{+}(T)\leq\sharp J
.

Remark 2.12
.

From now on we write
\mathfrak{S}:=\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern for the unit sphere in

.
For
m\in\mathbb{N}
let
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
be a rigid matrix
and consider the model operator

S_{\Theta}:K_{\Theta}\to K_{\Theta}
. It is clear that if
\Theta\equiv 0
then
S_{\Theta}

actually is the shift of multiplicity
m
and
\vartheta_{+}(S_{\Theta})=m
. This immediate observation
has an interesting generalization: if there is a sequence
z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}
such that

\Theta(z_{n})\to 0
then
\vartheta_{+}(S_{\Theta})=m
. Indeed, if

satisfies
(2) of Propos.

then

This is not possible if

. This shows that in Example

the hypothesis

is necessary for the frame number to be 1.

below.

If

admits a frame formed by finitely many orbits, there is some

such that

is similar to a model operator

acting on

for
some rigid matrix

. In this case, Theorem

tells us that

, there
exists some
\eta>0
satisfying (2) of Propos.
2.7
.
If
\Theta\equiv 0
(i.e.:
S_{\Theta}
is the shift of multiplicity
m
) then

F\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
must be invertible. For instance,
F
is the identity in the proof
of Theorem
2.1
.

The maximal ideal space of
H^{\infty}
is

\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})=\{\varphi:\mbox{$H^{\infty}$}\to\mathbb{C}\,\text{ linear, continuous, multiplicative and $\neq 0$}\}

with the weak
∗
topology, that makes it a compact Hausdorff space.
Via evaluations the disk can be viewed as an open subset of
\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})
and thanks to the corona theorem of
Carleson
[
7
]
we know that it is dense. Therefore
\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})
is a compactification of
\mathbb{D}
.
The Gelfand transform of
f\in\mbox{$H^{\infty}$}
consists of looking at
f
in the bidual space of
H^{\infty}
and
then restricting its domain to
\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})
. Thus, the Gelfand transform provides a continuous
extension of
f
to the whole
\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})
.
In particular, the Gelfand transform induces a continuous extension of
any
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
as a map from
\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})
into
\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
,
that we also denote by
\Theta
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
0\neq K_{\Theta}\subset(H^{2})^{m}
be the
S
-coinvariant subspace associated to the rigid matrix

\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
.
Then

\vartheta_{+}(S_{\Theta})\geq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})}\big(\!\dim\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta(x)\big).

Proof.

We are looking for the minimum integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that there are

and

satisfying

for all

and

, where

.
By the density of

in

and the compactness of the latter, this is equivalent to

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}\in\mathfrak{S}.

By the dimension theorem, the kernels of
\Theta(x)
and
\Theta^{\ast}(x)
have the same dimension.
Let
p
be the right number in (
8
) and
x\in\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})
be such that

\dim\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta^{\ast}(x)=p
. If
k
and
F
are such that (
7
) holds and we restrict

F^{\ast}(x)|_{\text{Ker}\,\Theta^{\ast}(x)}:\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta^{\ast}(x)\approx\mathbb{C}^{p}\to\mathbb{C}^{k},

the dimension theorem tells us that

p=\dim\big(\mbox{Ker}\,F^{\ast}(x)|_{\text{Ker}\,\Theta^{\ast}(x)}\big)+\dim\big(\mbox{Ran}\,F^{\ast}(x)|_{\text{Ker}\,\Theta^{\ast}(x)}\big),

where the last summand is
\leq k
. If
k<p
the first summand must be nonzero, which implies that

\mbox{Ker}\,F^{\ast}(x)\cap\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta^{\ast}(x)\neq 0
. Since this contradicts (
7
), we get
k\geq p
.
∎

The only reason for having a single inequality in Proposition
2.13
is that we were unable
to prove the other one. Below we conjecture the equality, which provides a formula for the unilateral
frame number. Furthermore, we show that it is enough to prove the conjecture
when
\Theta
is an inner matrix, and then we give an example that supports the conjecture.

Conjecture 2.14

(The unilateral frame number)
.

Let
0\neq K_{\Theta}\subset(H^{2})^{m}
be the
S
-coinvariant subspace associated to the rigid matrix

\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
.
Then

(8)

\vartheta_{+}(S_{\Theta})=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}(H^{\infty})}\big(\!\dim\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta(x)\big).

Remark 2.15
.

We shall see that it is enough to prove (
8
) when
\Theta
is inner.
Only the inequality (
\leq
) needs to be proved.
Denote by
E\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
the common initial space of the partial isometries

\Theta(e^{it})
for almost every
e^{it}\in\partial\mathbb{D}
. If
\Theta
is not an inner matrix,

m_{1}:=\dim E<m
. Then there is an unitary matrix
V\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
mapping

\mathbb{C}^{m_{1}}\times\{0\}^{m-m_{1}}
onto
E
and
\{0\}^{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{C}^{m-m_{1}}
onto
E^{\bot}
.
Consequently,

\Theta V=\begin{bmatrix}\Theta_{1}&\hskip 0.0pt0\\
\!\!0&\hskip 0.0pt0\\
\end{bmatrix}\in(\mbox{$H^{\infty}$})^{m\times m},\text{ where }\Theta_{1}\in(\mbox{$H^{\infty}$})^{m_{1}\times m_{1}}\text{ is inner.}

Therefore, if there is
F_{1}\in(\mbox{$H^{\infty}$})^{m_{1}\times k_{1}}
(with
k_{1}\leq m_{1}
)
such that
F_{1},\,\Theta_{1}
satisfy (
7
) with
k_{1}
and
m_{1}
instead of
k
and
m
, the pair

F=\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}&\hskip-8.53581pt0\\
\!\!0&I_{m-m_{1}}\\
\end{bmatrix}\in(\mbox{$H^{\infty}$})^{m\times(k_{1}+m-m_{1})}\,\mbox{ and $\Theta V$ satisfy \eqref{maxcoro},}

and the same holds for the pair
F
and
\Theta
.

By the above reduction, to prove the conjecture we can assume that
\Theta
is an inner matrix,
and consequently its determinant is an inner function. We give below an example where, in addition,
the conjecture is true.

Example 2.16
.

The conjecture holds if
\Theta\in(H^{\infty})^{m\times m}
is such that
u:=\det\Theta
is
an interpolating Blaschke product.

.

), for each

Since
Z_{u}
is compact, there is a finite covering by these sets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{N}
,
and the associated matrices

, with
1\leq j\leq N
, such that for some

,

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern Furthermore, since
Z_{u}
is totally disconnected (see
[
11
, p. 395]
), by a well-known result of
dimension theory (see
[
17
, p. 87]
) we can assume that the
V_{j}^{\prime}s
satisfy the extra condition

(V_{j}\cap Z_{u})\cap(V_{k}\cap Z_{u})=\emptyset\ \mbox{ if }j\neq k.

This means that every
y\in Z_{u}
belongs to only one of the
V_{j}^{\prime}s
.
In particular, when
z_{n}\in Z_{u}\cap\mathbb{D}
let
V_{j}
be the corresponding set such that
z_{n}\in V_{j}
.
Since the sequence
\{z_{n}\}
is interpolating, there is
F\in(\mbox{$H^{\infty}$})^{m\times p}

such that
F(z_{n})=F_{j}
. By the density of
Z_{u}\cap\mathbb{D}
in
Z_{u}
(see
[
11
, p. 395]
), for every
x\in Z_{u}
:

F(x)=F_{j},\mbox{ where $V_{j}$ is the unique set that contains $x$.}

Consequently, (
9
) says that

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\ \mbox{ when $y\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}V_{j}$,}

and since this is an open neighborhood of

, we also have

when
x\not\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}V_{j}
.
That is, (
7
) holds for
F\in(\mbox{$H^{\infty}$})^{m\times p}
, giving the inequality that is missing in the
conjecture.

In the above example,
u
could be a finite Blaschke product that satisfies finite interpolation
(i.e.: with single zeros). Moreover, it is clear that the argument in the Example can be easily adapted
to a finite Blaschke product times an interpolating Blaschke product.

3.
Bilateral orbits

Let
J
be a countable set of indexes and let
\mathbb{Z}
be the set of integers.
Denote by
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
the space of
L^{2}
functions from
\mathbb{T}
into
\ell^{2}(J)
.
That is, any
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
writes as (
1
),
where the sum runs over
\mathbb{Z}
and
z\in\mathbb{T}
.
The bilateral shift
U
of multiplicity
\sharp J
is the operator of multiplication by the variable
z

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
. It is clear that
U^{\ast}
consists of multiplication by

z^{-1}=\overline{z}
, so
U
is unitary.
A subspace
E\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
is doubly invariant
if
E
and
E^{\bot}
are
U
-invariant, or equivalently,
E
is both
U
and
U^{\ast}
invariant.
A proof analogous to that of Theorem
2.1
gives

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
be an invertible operator and
f_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
,
where
j\in J
is countable.
Then
\{T^{n}f_{j}:n\in\mathbb{Z},\ j\in J\}
is a frame for
\mathcal{H}
if and only if there is
a doubly invariant subspace
K\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
and an invertible operator
V:K\to\mathcal{H}

such that

V^{-1}TV=U|_{K}\ \mbox{ and }\ V(P_{K}e_{j})=f_{j}\ \mbox{ for all $j\in J$},

where
e_{j}\in\ell^{2}(J)
is the standard basis.

The theorem relates general frames of bilateral orbits to doubly invariant subspaces of

L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\ell^{2}(J))
. The characterization of these spaces were obtained by Helson and Lowdenslager
[
13
]
and
Srinivasan
[
18
]
.
We follow the treatment of Lecture VI in Helson’s book
[
14
]
.

Definition.
A range function is a map defined almost everywhere

\mathcal{J}:\mathbb{T}\to\{\mbox{closed subspaces of $\mathcal{H}$}\}

that is (weakly) measurable in the sense that if
P_{\mathcal{J}(z)}
is the orthogonal projection on

\mathcal{J}(z)
, then
z\mapsto\langle P_{\mathcal{J}(z)}a,b\rangle
is measurable for all
a,b\in\mathcal{H}
.

In
[
14
, Thm. 8]
it is showed that a closed subspace
\mathfrak{M}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{H})
is
doubly invariant if and only if there is a (unique) measurable range function
\mathcal{J}

such that

\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{M}_{\mathcal{J}}:=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{H}):\ f(z)\in\mathcal{J}(z),\mbox{ a.e.}\ z\in\mathbb{T}\}.

So, the orthogonal projection
P_{\mathfrak{M}}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{H})\to\mathfrak{M}
is given by

(P_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}})(z)=P_{\mathcal{J}(z)}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}(z)\ \mbox{ for a.e.$\,z\in\mathbb{T}$}.

When
\mathcal{H}=\ell^{2}(J)
, where
\sharp J=m\in\mathbb{N}
, we identify
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{H})=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C})^{m}=(L^{2})^{m}
,
where the last equality is just notation.
Therefore for
\mbox{a.e.}\,z\in\mathbb{T}
, the orthogonal projection

P_{\mathcal{J}(z)}:\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathcal{J}(z)
is represented by
a multiplication operator
M_{\sigma(z)}
whose symbol is pointwise the matrix of a
projection in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.
That is, there is a
unique
matrix
\sigma\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times m}

such that
M_{\sigma}=P_{\mathfrak{M}}:(L^{2})^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
.
So,
P_{\mathcal{J}(z)}=M_{\sigma(z)}
for a.e.
\,z\in\mathbb{T}
.
Conversely, if
\sigma\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times m}
is any self-adjoint and idempotent matrix then

\mathfrak{M}:=M_{\sigma}(L^{2})^{m}
is a doubly invariant subspace.
It follows immediately that
M_{\sigma}(L^{\infty})^{m}=(L^{\infty})^{m}\cap\mathfrak{M}
is dense in
\mathfrak{M}
.

3.1.
Multiplication operators on doubly invariant subspaces

Most of the results in this subsection are completely analogous to those for Toeplitz operators
on model subspaces, except that the multiplication operator is not followed by the analytic projection.
This greatly simplifies many arguments.

For
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
L^{2}=L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
G\in(L^{2})^{m\times k}
define the multiplication operator

It is densely defined on

and if we write

, it is immediate that

is bounded on

if and only if the scalar operators
M_{g_{ij}}

are bounded on

, which is equivalent to
g_{ij}\in L^{\infty}
for all

.
Also,
M^{\ast}_{G}=M_{G^{\ast}}
.

Proof.

Let

be the matrix that satisfies
M_{\sigma}=P_{\mathfrak{M}}
, the orthogonal projection.

that form a Bessel sequence, or a frame for its closed span in terms of the fibers

\Phi(z)=\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g\hfil$\crcr}}}_{j}(z):\ 1\leq j\leq k,\ z\in\mathbb{T}\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}.

Namely,
\mathcal{F}
is a Bessel sequence with constant
B
or a frame with constants
A,\ B

if and only if the same holds for
\Phi(z)
uniformly for a.e
z\in\mathbb{T}
.
Given a doubly invariant subspace
\mathfrak{M}\subset(L^{2})^{m}
,
Theorem
3.4
below gives a characterization of the Bessel sequences and the frames
in
\mathfrak{M}
in terms of the multiplication operator that projects on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\,\mathfrak{M}\subset(L^{2})^{m}
be a doubly invariant subspace and
\beta\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times m}

such that
M_{\beta}=P_{\mathfrak{M}^{\bot}}
.
If
F\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times k}
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

M_{FF^{\ast}}\geq\delta^{2}I_{(L^{2})^{m}}
on
\mathfrak{M}
for some
\delta>0
.

(2)

F(z)F^{\ast}(z)+\beta(z)\geq\eta^{2}I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

for some
\eta>0
and a.e.
\,z\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{M}=\mbox{Ker}\,M_{\beta}
, it is clear that (1) is equivalent to

M_{FF^{\ast}}+M_{\beta}\geq\varepsilon^{2}I_{(L^{2})^{m}}
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
Also, this is an immediate consequence of (2).
Finally, if this inequality holds, consider a function
\chi_{E}(z)\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern where

denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}
. Since

is arbitrary, we conclude that
(2) holds with
\eta=\varepsilon
.
∎

.
The bilateral frame number of a model operator on
\mathfrak{M}\subset(L^{2})^{m}
can be obtained from

[
4
, Thm. 3.3]
(see also the subsequent Remark (i) in
[
4

).
We give below a very simple proof that exploits a classical diagonalization result.

Theorem 3.5

(The bilateral frame number)
.

Let
0\neq\mathfrak{M}\subset(L^{2})^{m}
be a doubly invariant subspace and

\Theta\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times m}
be the matrix that satisfies
M_{\Theta}=P_{\mathfrak{M}^{\bot}}
.
Then

\vartheta_{-}(U|_{\mathfrak{M}})=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{T}}\big(\!\dim\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta(z)\big),\vskip-5.69054pt

where
\sup
is the essential supremum and
\Theta(z)
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
for a.e.
\,z\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

Since
\Theta
is self-adjoint, a result of Kadison
[
15
, Thm. 3.19]
asserts that
there is a unitary matrix
V\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times m}
such that
V^{\ast}\Theta V=D\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times m}

is the diagonal matrix of a projection for a.e.
\,z\in\mathbb{T}
.
Therefore, when
G\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times k}
, we have

where
F\in(L^{\infty})^{m\times k}
.
Hence, if
\mathfrak{D}\subset(L^{2})^{m}
is the doubly invariant subspace that satisfies
M_{D}=P_{\mathfrak{D}^{\bot}}
,
we get
\vartheta_{-}(U|_{\mathfrak{M}})=\vartheta_{-}(U|_{\mathfrak{D}})
.
The diagonal of
D
is formed by the characteristic functions of measurable sets

E_{1},\ldots,E_{m}\subset\mathbb{T}
.

G^{\ast}
must satisfy

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\hfil$\crcr}}}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}\geq\eta\ \mbox{ for a.e.$\,z\in\mathbb{T}$ and some $\eta>0$},

the rank of
G^{\ast}(z)
must be at least the cardinal of
\{1\leq j\leq m:\ \chi_{E_{j}}(z)=0\}
. Thus,

\vartheta_{-}(U|_{\mathfrak{D}})\geq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{T}}\big(\!\dim\mbox{Ker}\,D(z)\big).\vskip-2.84526pt

For the other inequality let
p
be the above supremum and let
1\leq j_{1}<\ldots<j_{t}\leq m
(with
t\leq p
),
be such that
\chi_{E_{j_{i}}}(z)=0
.
Then the matrix
G^{\ast}\in(L^{\infty})^{p\times m}
whose rows are

, where
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is the standard basis,
satisfies (
14
). Hence,

\vartheta_{-}(U|_{\mathfrak{M}})=\vartheta_{-}(U|_{\mathfrak{D}})=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{T}}\big(\!\dim\mbox{Ker}\,D(z)\big)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{T}}\big(\!\dim\mbox{Ker}\,\Theta(z)\big).\vskip-19.91692pt

∎
